T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{i\omega_{n}}\frac{1}{i\omega_{n}-\xi_{m_{1},-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{-i\omega_{n}-\xi_{m_{2},\eta,-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}=-\frac{n_{F}(\xi_{m_{1},-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})-n_{F}(-\xi_{m_{2},\eta,-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}{\xi_{m_{1},-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\xi_{m_{2},\eta,-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}.

(354)

In the flat band limit, we have
\xi_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}\eta}=\xi_{\mathbf{k}}\ll k_{B}T
, and up to the lowest order term, we find

T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\approx\frac{\beta}{4},

(355)

which is independent of
m_{1},m_{2},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta
. In this limit, the gap equation is simplified to

\Delta_{e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2};\mathbf{k}}^{\eta}=\frac{\beta}{2N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2}}V^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left[P_{m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}\Delta^{-\eta}_{s_{1}s_{2},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}P^{T}_{m_{2},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta}\right]_{e_{1}e_{2}}.

(356)

Furthermore, one can show that

\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}}P_{m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}\Delta^{-\eta}_{s_{1}s_{2},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}P^{T}_{m_{2},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta}=\Delta^{-\eta}_{s_{1}s_{2},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

(357)

and thus in the flat band limit, the linearized gap equation is simplified as

2k_{B}T\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{-\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}},

(358)

where we denote
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta_{e_{1}\eta s_{1},e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}(\mathbf{k})
. The above equation can be written in an eigen-problem
form

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}\\
\Delta^{-\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}\\
\Delta^{-\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\\
V^{-\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}&0\end{array}\right)

needs to be diagonalized and possesses a off-diagonal form.

We can decompose the gap function into the orbital part and spin part. Let’s denote the spin part as
{\mathcal{S}}^{SM}_{s_{1}s_{2}}
, where
S=0,1
and
M=-S,...,S
.
Explicitly,
{\mathcal{S}}^{00}=is_{y}
for spin singlet and
{\mathcal{S}}^{1,\pm 1}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(s_{x}\pm is_{y})s_{y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-s_{z}\mp s_{0}),{\mathcal{S}}^{1,0}=s_{x}
for spin triplet, where
s_{0}
is the identity matrix and
s_{x,y,z}
are three Pauli matrices.
Then we can decompose

\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\sum_{S,M}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}{\mathcal{S}}^{SM}_{s_{1}s_{2}}
. Since
V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{kk^{\prime}}
is independent of spin
and
Tr(({\mathcal{S}}^{SM})^{\dagger}{\mathcal{S}}^{S^{\prime}M^{\prime}})=2\delta_{SS^{\prime}}\delta_{MM^{\prime}}
, the eigen-equation for the gap function is

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\\
\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}\\
\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}\end{array}\right)

for any
S,M
.

Since we only have two independent
V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
for the fix
\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
, namely one complex
V^{+++}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
one real
V^{++-}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, we can explicitly write down two independent equations explicitly, namely,

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};++}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};++}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{+,++}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};++}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};++}\end{array}\right),\;\;\mathcal{V}^{+,++}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&V^{+++}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\\
V^{-++}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}&0\end{array}\right),

and

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};+-}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{+,+-}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};+-}\end{array}\right),\;\;\mathcal{V}^{+,+-}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&V^{++-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\\
V^{-+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}&0\end{array}\right).

With the hermitian and reshuffling conditions, one can show
V^{\eta,e_{Y},e_{Y}^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=(V^{-\eta,e_{Y},e_{Y}^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}})^{*}
, or more explicitly,

V^{-++}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=(V^{+++}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}})^{*}
and
V^{-+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=(V^{++-}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}})^{*}
,
so both equations are the eigen-equations for hermitian matrices
\mathcal{V}^{+,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
,
which can be solved numerically through diagonalization process.

Now let us discuss several properties of the gap function
\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}
.
Although some results discussed below have been derived in Sec.
VI.7.1
from the symmetry view of the
gap function, we will show similar results can be obtained from the linearized gap equation (
VI.8.1
).
With the reshuffling symmetry Eq. (
291
),
{\mathcal{S}}^{00}_{s_{1}s_{2}}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{00}_{s_{2}s_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{1M}_{s_{1}s_{2}}={\mathcal{S}}^{1M}_{s_{2}s_{1}}
,
we have

\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{-\eta,00}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{2}e_{1}}

(386)

for spin singlet and

\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=-\Delta^{-\eta,1M}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{2}e_{1}}

(387)

for spin triplet.

As well known for normal superconductors, when there is inversion symmetry, the orbital part of the gap function has definite parity, and
we only have spin-singlet even-parity or spin-triplet odd-parity gap functions. There are two differences for TBG: (1)
There is no inversion symmetry for TBG and thus the
spinless

C_{2z}
symmetry plays the role of inversion; and (2) Due to the
existence of Chern-band index, spin-singlet odd-parity or triplet even-parity states can also exist. Below we will discuss these two points in details.

Let us first consider the
\hat{C}_{2z}
symmetry, which requires
V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=V^{-\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
. Thus, the gap
equation (
358
) can be transformed as

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}\rightarrow 2k_{B}T\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{-\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{-\mathbf{k},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow 2k_{B}T\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}

(388)

In the above derivation, we replace
\mathbf{k}\rightarrow-\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta\rightarrow-\eta
in the first step and use
C_{2z}
symmetry in the
second step. We can write the above equation into an eigen-equation form

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\\
\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}\\
\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime};e_{1}e_{2}}\end{array}\right),

which can be compared with Eq. (
VI.8.1
), and one can see that

\displaystyle\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{-\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

(394)

with
|\lambda_{C_{2z}}|=1
for any
S,M
.
If the above procedure is performed twice, we need to go back to the original equation, so
\lambda_{C_{2z}}^{2}=1
, which means
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=\pm 1
.
This can be understood as the definite parity
\pm 1
for the gap function with respect to
\hat{C}_{2z}
symmetry.

We combine the anti-symmetry condition (Eq. (
386
) and (
387
))
with the definite parity of the orbital part Eq. (
394
) and obtain

\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};e_{2}e_{1}}

(395)

for spin singlet and

\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=-\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};e_{2}e_{1}}

(396)

for spin triplet.

Next let us consider the role of Chern-band index
e_{1,2}
in the gap function. We decompose the gap function into

\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\sum_{\mu,SM}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}(\zeta^{\mu})_{e_{1}e_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}^{SM})_{s_{1}s_{2}},

(397)

where
\mu=0,x,y,z
and
\zeta^{\mu}
are the identity and Pauli matrices for the Chern-band index. The total gap function
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}

needs to be anti-symmetric. For intra-band channels,
\zeta^{\mu}
should be
\zeta^{0}
or
\zeta^{z}
, both of which are symmetric matrices. So to make the total gap function being anti-symmetric, the singlet channel
\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}
can only be even-parity (
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=+
) and the triplet channel
\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}
can only be odd-parity (
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=-
) for intra-Chern-band pairings.
This is consistent with the standard spin-singlet even-parity or spin-triplet odd-parity pairings.

For inter-Chern-band channels,
\zeta^{\mu}
can be symmetric
\zeta^{x}
or anti-symmetric
\zeta^{y}
. To make the total gap function anti-symmetric, the singlet channel
\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}
can either be even-parity for
\mu=x
as
\zeta^{x}
is symmetric, or odd-parity for
\mu=y
as
\zeta^{y}
is anti-symmetric. Similarly, the triplet channel
\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}
can be either even-parity for
\mu=y
or odd-parity for
\mu=x
.
Next we will decompose the linearized gap equation (
358
) into the
\zeta^{x}
and
\zeta^{y}
channels and show that they have the same form. We substitute the decomposition (
397
) into Eq. (
358
) and with the orthonormal relation
Tr(\zeta^{\mu}\zeta^{\nu})=2\delta_{\mu\nu}
, we find

4k_{B}T\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};\nu}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mu}\left(\sum_{e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\zeta^{\mu})_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{\nu})_{e_{2}e_{1}}\right)\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mu}.

(398)

For the inter-Chern-band channels, we take
\mu,\nu
to be
x,y
and find

\sum_{e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\zeta^{\mu})_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{\mu})_{e_{2}e_{1}}=V^{\eta+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+V^{\eta-+}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=2V^{\eta+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},\;\mu=x,y

(399)

\sum_{e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\zeta^{x})_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{y})_{e_{2}e_{1}}=\sum_{e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{e_{1},-e_{2}}(-i)\epsilon_{e_{2}e_{1}}=(-i)(-V^{\eta+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+V^{\eta-+}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})=0

(400)

and

\sum_{e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\zeta^{y})_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{x})_{e_{2}e_{1}}=0,

(401)

where we have used
V^{\eta+-}_{kk^{\prime}}=V^{\eta-+}_{kk^{\prime}}
from the constraints of the
V
function in Eqs. (
VI.4
).
With the above relations, we obtain

2k_{B}T\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};\mu}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mu}V^{\eta+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mu},\;\;\mu=x,y.

(402)

Thus,
\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};x}
and
\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k};y}
satisfy the same linearized gap equation.
The largest eigen-value of the above gap equation, which determines the
T_{c}
for the inter-Chern-band channel, has a certain
C_{2z}
-parity,
say even-parity, and then both singlet channel
\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};\mu=x}
and triplet channel
\Delta^{\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k};\mu=y}
can exist and are
degenerate with the same
T_{c}
. This is consistent with the conclusion that singlet and triplet channels are degenerate for the inter-Chern-band pairings in the chiral flat-band limit from the continuous symmetry analysis in Sec.
VI.7.2
.

VI.8.2
Intra-Chern-band channels from linearized gap equation

Now let us discuss the intra-Chern-band channels. Besides Eq. (
VI.8.1
), we have another linearized gap equation for
V^{\pm,--}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};--}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};--}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&V^{+,--}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\\
V^{-,--}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};--}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};--}.\end{array}\right),

which is directly related to Eq. (
VI.8.1
) by symmetry.
Since
C_{2z}T
requires
V^{\eta,e_{Y},e^{\prime}_{Y}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=(V^{\eta,-e_{Y},-e_{Y}^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{*}
, the matrix
\mathcal{V}^{+,--}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is just the complex conjugate of that in Eq. (
VI.8.1
). Therefore, we expect (1) The eigen-equations (
VI.8.1
)
and (
VI.8.2
) give the same
T_{c}
, and (2) the corresponding eigen-vectors are related by

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};--}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};--}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};++}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};++}.\end{array}\right)^{*}

up to an arbitrary phase factor, which can always be chosen to be
1
. For the convenience of the notation, we define

\displaystyle\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e^{\prime}_{Y}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{Y}e^{\prime}_{Y}}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k};e_{Y}e^{\prime}_{Y}}\end{array}\right)

so the above relation can be simplified as

\displaystyle\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},--}=(\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},++})^{*}.

(413)

Before we show the numerical results, we first consider some simplified version for the analytical results. Let us
consider the phonon-mediated el-el interaction with the following form for the intra-Chern-band channel
Song and Bernevig 2022
;
liu

\displaystyle V^{\eta,e_{Y},e_{Y}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=U^{*}_{0,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}U_{0,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+U^{*}_{1,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}U_{1,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}};\;U_{0,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}=\frac{\sqrt{A}b^{2}}{k^{2}+b^{2}};\;U_{1,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}=\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}}{k^{2}+b^{2}}k_{e_{Y}}^{2};\;\;k_{e_{Y}}=k_{x}+ie_{Y}k_{y},

(414)

where
e_{Y}=\pm
and
A
,
V_{0}
and
b
are material dependent parameters with their values
A=0.44meV
,
V_{0}=0.4meV
and
b=0.185k_{\theta}
obtained from the heavy fermion model for TBG.
We note that
U_{0}
is independent of
e_{Y}
index (for s-wave) but the form of
U_{1}
critically depends on
e_{Y}
index (for d-wave). Both
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
are independent of the valley
\eta
index. The corresponding linearized gap equation for intra-Chern-band channel is written as

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\alpha}U_{\alpha,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}U^{*}_{\alpha,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\end{array}\right),

where
\alpha=0,1
. The above decomposition form of the interaction allows us to define

\tilde{\Delta}^{\eta,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}U_{\alpha,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{Y}e_{Y}}

(422)

for the intra-Chern-band channel and simplify the gap equation as

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{\alpha}U^{*}_{\alpha,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\end{array}\right).

Now we can introduce

\tilde{V}_{\beta\alpha,e_{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}U_{\beta,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}}U^{*}_{\alpha,e_{Y},\mathbf{k}},

(430)

and transform the gap equation into an eigen-problem

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\beta,e_{Y}}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\beta,e_{Y}}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{V}_{\beta\alpha,e_{Y}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\end{array}\right).

Crucially, we find that

\tilde{V}_{01,-e_{Y}}=\tilde{V}_{10,e_{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{\sqrt{AV_{0}}b^{2}k_{e_{Y}}^{2}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}}=0

(438)

after performing the angular integral of the momentum. Thus, two channels (s-wave and d-wave) are decoupled,

\tilde{V}_{\beta\alpha,e_{Y}}=\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha,e_{Y}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}
. Furthermore, we find

\tilde{V}_{00,e_{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{Ab^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}},\;\;\tilde{V}_{11,e_{Y}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{V_{0}k^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}},

(439)

both of which are independent of the parameter
e_{Y}
, so we drop the index
e_{Y}
for
\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha}
(
\alpha=0,1
) below.

Now the gap equation becomes

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha}\\
\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,e_{Y}}\end{array}\right),

with
e_{Y}=\pm
and
\alpha=0,1
for s-wave and d-wave channels, separately. This eigen-problem can be easily solved
with two eigen-solutions
\pm\frac{\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha}}{2}
, and we only take the positive value one, namely

k_{B}T_{c,\alpha}=\frac{\tilde{V}_{\alpha\alpha}}{2}.

(447)

To get an estimate of
T_{c}
in each channel, We can change the momentum summation in Eq. (
439
) to the momentum integral with the cut-off
\sim k_{\theta}
and obtain

\tilde{V}_{00}=\frac{S_{M}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{k_{\theta}}kdk\frac{Ab^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}},\;\;\tilde{V}_{11}=\frac{S_{M}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{k_{\theta}}kdk\frac{V_{0}k^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}}

(448)

where
S_{M}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}a_{M}^{2}
,
a_{M}
is the Moire lattice constant

Numerical integral over the momentum gives
\tilde{V}_{00}\approx 0.018meV
and
\tilde{V}_{11}\approx 0.387meV
, which give rise to

k_{B}T_{c,0}\approx 0.009meV
for s-wave channel and
k_{B}T_{c,1}\approx 0.193meV
for the d-wave channel. Based on this estimate, we conclude that
the d-wave pairing channel will win and give the critical temperature

k_{B}T_{c}^{intra}=\frac{\tilde{V}_{11}}{2}\approx 0.193meV

(449)

for the intra-Chern-band channel.

The corresponding normalized eigen-vector is
(\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{+},\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{+})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,1)
.
With the normalized eigen-vector and Eq. (
VI.8.2
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},++}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},++}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},++}\end{array}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}}{\sqrt{2}\tilde{V}_{11}(k^{2}+b^{2})}(k_{x}-ik_{y})^{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
1\end{array}\right).

We also consider the gap equation (
VI.8.2
), which should given the same
T_{c}
, and the corresponding eigen-state is given by

\displaystyle\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},--}=(\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},++})^{*}=\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}}{\sqrt{2}\tilde{V}_{11}(k^{2}+b^{2})}(k_{x}+ik_{y})^{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
1\end{array}\right).

according to Eq. (
413
). It should be noted that both
\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},++}
and
\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},--}
have
even parity,
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=1
, so they can only form spin-singlet pairing (
S,M=0,0
in the above gap function).

We emphasize that
\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},++}
and
\Psi^{SM}_{\mathbf{k},--}
are two independent degenerate pairing channels with the same
T_{c}
,
and they together form a 2D
E_{2}
irreducible representation. To see that, we need to consider the operator form of the gap function and define

\displaystyle H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},s_{1},s_{2},\eta}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y},\eta,s_{1}}^{\dagger}\Delta^{\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k};e_{Y}e_{Y}}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}}\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y},-\eta,s_{2}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2V_{0}}}{\tilde{V}_{0}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},s_{1},s_{2}}\frac{k_{-e_{Y}}^{2}}{k^{2}+b^{2}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y},+,s_{1}}^{\dagger}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}}\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y},-,s_{2}}^{\dagger}

(458)

where
e_{Y}=\pm
.
Here
H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}
only involves the creation operator terms, so the full gap Hamiltonian should be
H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}+H^{\dagger}_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}
to keep the Hamiltonian hermitian.
Now we want to check the transformation property of
H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}
under
C_{3z}
and
C_{2x}
(spinless).
Since we are dealing with the continuous model around
\mathbf{k}=0
(excluding Moiré BZ boundary), we should be able to choose the phase factors

\theta(\mathbf{k})=\alpha(\mathbf{k})=0
for
C_{3z}
and
C_{2x}
in Eq. (
VI.7
), so

C_{3z}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}\eta s}^{\dagger}C_{3z}^{-1}=\gamma_{C_{3}\mathbf{k},e_{Y}\eta s}^{\dagger}
and

C_{2x}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}\eta s}^{\dagger}C_{2x}^{-1}=\gamma_{C_{2x}\mathbf{k},-e_{Y}\eta s}^{\dagger}
.
Then, one can show

C_{3z}H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}C_{3z}^{-1}=e^{ie_{Y}\frac{2\pi}{3}}H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}};C_{2x}H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}C_{2x}^{-1}=H_{\Delta_{-e_{Y}}}.

(459)

We further define

H_{\Delta_{1}}=H_{\Delta_{+}}+H_{\Delta_{-}};H_{\Delta_{2}}=i(H_{\Delta_{+}}-H_{\Delta_{-}})

(460)

and on this basis
(H_{\Delta_{1}},H_{\Delta_{2}})
, we have

C_{3z}H_{\Delta_{\alpha}}C_{3z}^{-1}=\sum_{\beta}H_{\Delta_{\beta}}(e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{y}})_{\beta\alpha};C_{2x}H_{\Delta_{\alpha}}C_{2x}^{-1}=\sum_{\beta}H_{\Delta_{\beta}}(\sigma_{z})_{\beta\alpha},

(461)

where
\sigma
is the Pauli matrix for two components of pairings.
Since
(H_{\Delta_{1}},H_{\Delta_{2}})
is even under
C_{2z}
,
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=1
, we conclude
(H_{\Delta_{1}},H_{\Delta_{2}})
belongs to the 2D
E_{2}
irrep
by comparing with the irrep table (Tab.
4
).
It should be emphasized that
H_{\Delta_{\alpha}}
(
\alpha=1,2
)
cannot
be written in the
k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}
or
k_{x}k_{y}
form because of the Chern-band index.
For example, the explicit form of
H_{\Delta_{1}}
is

\displaystyle H_{\Delta_{1}}=H_{\Delta_{+}}+H_{\Delta_{-}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2V_{0}}}{\tilde{V}_{0}N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},s_{1},s_{2}}\frac{1}{k^{2}+b^{2}}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}}\left(k_{-}^{2}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},+,+,s_{1}}^{\dagger}\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},+,-,s_{2}}^{\dagger}+k_{+}^{2}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},-,+,s_{1}}^{\dagger}\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},-,-,s_{2}}^{\dagger}\right),

(462)

and it is clear that we cannot make the summation over
k_{-}^{2}
and
k_{+}^{2}
to get
k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}
form.

The above analytical solutions fit well with our numerical results as discussed below. The largest three eigen-values of the linearized gap equations (Eqs.
VI.8.1
and
VI.8.1
) are shown in Tab.
5
. The largest eigen-value of the intra-Chern-band channel
correspond to
k_{B}T_{c}=0.16meV
, which is quite close to the value of
k_{B}T_{c}\approx 0.19meV
obtained from the effective interaction discussed above.

Intra-Chern-band channel

0.16 meV

0.04 meV

0.0002 meV

Inter-Chern-band channel

0.21 meV

0.008 meV

0.0079 meV

Table 5:

k_{B}T
for the largest three eigen-values of the linearized gap equations (Eqs.
VI.8.1
and
VI.8.1
).
The largest one determines
k_{B}T_{c}
.

We also look at the eigen-vector of the intra-Chern-band channel with the largest eigen-value and show that our numerical result also gives
the 2D
E_{2}
irrep for this channel.
Fig.
15
(a) shows the gap function
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
as a function of the momentum
\mathbf{k}
, in which the superconducting gap closes,
thus revealing a nodal structure at
\Gamma_{M}
. This is consistent with the 2D
E_{2}
irrep, as shown by Eq. (
309
) or Eq. (
VI.8.2
).

To further confirm this conclusion, we test the transformation property of
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
under
C_{3z}
rotation. From Eq. (
459
),
we expect

e^{-ie_{Y}(\theta(\mathbf{k})+\theta(-\mathbf{k}))}\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}=e^{ie_{Y}\frac{2\pi}{3}}\Delta^{\eta}_{C_{3z}\mathbf{k},e_{Y}e_{Y}}

(463)

for the 2D
E_{2}
irrep channel. We want to test the above expression numerically. We numerically calculate
\theta(\mathbf{k})
from the eigen-state of the flat bands
in the BM model and solve
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
from the linearized gap equation. With
\theta(\mathbf{k})
and
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
, we can compute the phase factor
\Phi_{+}(\mathbf{k})
, defined as

e^{i\Phi_{+}(\mathbf{k})}=\frac{e^{-i(\theta(\mathbf{k})+\theta(-\mathbf{k}))}\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}}{\Delta^{\eta}_{C_{3z}\mathbf{k},++}},

(464)

which is shown as a function of
\mathbf{k}
in Fig.
15
(c). One can see that the phase
\Phi_{+}
is always

\frac{2\pi}{3}
except around
\mathbf{k}\sim 0
, where
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
is zero. This confirms that our numerical results satisfy the transformation property Eq. (
459
)
that corresponds to the 2D
E_{2}
irrep pairing channel.

As a comparison, we also consider
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
with the next largest eigen-value, as shown in Fig.
15
(b). In this case, we
find a node existing at
\mathbf{K}_{M}
, which implies that the next highest
T_{c}
channel belongs to the 1D irrep. Indeed, Fig.
15
(d) shows
that
\Phi_{+}\sim 0
for the next highest
T_{c}
channel (except around
\mathbf{K}_{M}
where
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}\sim 0
), and thus we should have

C_{3z}H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}C_{3z}^{-1}=H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}};C_{2x}H_{\Delta_{e_{Y}}}C_{2x}^{-1}=H_{\Delta_{-e_{Y}}}

(465)

in this case. By performing the transformation (
460
), we obtain

C_{3z}H_{\Delta_{\alpha}}C_{3z}^{-1}=H_{\Delta_{\alpha}};C_{2x}H_{\Delta_{\alpha}}C_{2x}^{-1}=\sum_{\beta}H_{\Delta_{\beta}}(\sigma_{z})_{\beta\alpha}.

(466)

Thus,
H_{\Delta_{1}}
and
H_{\Delta_{2}}
belong to 1D
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
irreps, respectively, and they are degenerate.

To understand the above results, we come back to the gap equation.
In the chiral flat band limit, as we have seen, the linearized gap equation that contains both channels of the intra-Chern-band pairing has the following diagonal form

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi^{SM}_{++}\\
\Psi^{SM}_{--}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{V}^{+,++}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{V}^{+,--}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi^{SM}_{++}\\
\Psi^{SM}_{--}\end{array}\right),

in which two opposite Chern-band channels do not mix with each other. This form of the el-el interaction can be traced back to the chiral symmetry and flat band approximation used in our derivation.
With such diagonal form, the linearized gap equation shares a U(1) symmetry created by the generator
\sigma_{z}
, which acts on the basis of
(\Psi^{SM}_{++},\Psi^{SM}_{--})
. On the other hand,
C_{2x}
symmetry acts as the Pauli matrix
\sigma_{x}
on the above basis (See Eq.
459
).
As
\{\sigma_{x},\sigma_{z}\}=0
(anti-commutation relation), the eigen-state of the above eigen-problem Eq. (
VI.8.2
) must be doubly degenerate.

Figure 15:
(a) and (b) depict the gap function
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},++}
for the largest (
k_{B}T_{c}=0.16meV
) and next largest (
k_{B}T_{c}=0.04meV
)
eigen-values of the linearized gap equation,
respectively, for the intra-Chern-band channels.
(c) and (d) show the corresponding phase factor
\Phi_{+}(\mathbf{k})/\pi
due to
C_{3z}
rotation of the gap function.

VI.8.3
Inter-Chern-band channels from linearized gap equation

Next we consider the inter-Chern-band channel, which is determined by the linearized gap equation (
VI.8.1
). The gap function for
the highest
T_{c}
as a function of
\mathbf{k}
is shown in Fig.
16
(a), in which one can easily see that it is almost a constant over the whole Moiré BZ,
so it is s-wave pairing (Fig.
16
(b)).
Furthermore, one can show the parity of
C_{2z}
for the gap function is
\lambda_{Cz}=1
and the phase of
C_{3z}
rotation
\Phi_{+-}
is 0, so we
expect this gap function belongs to the
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
irreps. As discussed in the early part, we should be able to construct both spin
singlet and triplet pairings, which are degenerate, for the inter-Chern-band channels.

To see that more explicitly, we may consider the form of el-el from the heavy fermion model,

\displaystyle V^{\eta,e_{Y},-e_{Y}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=U^{*}_{0,\mathbf{k}}U_{0,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+U^{*}_{1,\mathbf{k}}U_{1,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}};U_{0,\mathbf{k}}=\frac{\sqrt{A}b^{2}}{k^{2}+b^{2}};U_{1,\mathbf{k}}=\frac{\sqrt{V_{0}}k^{2}}{k^{2}+b^{2}}.

(474)

The corresponding linearized gap equation for inter-Chern-band channel is then written as

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\alpha}U_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}U^{*}_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},+-}\end{array}\right),

where
\alpha=0,1
. Now we define

\tilde{\Delta}^{\eta,SM}_{\alpha,+-}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}U_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},+-}

(482)

and rewrite the linearized gap equation as

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{\alpha}U^{*}_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\end{array}\right).

By defining

\tilde{V}_{\beta\alpha}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}U_{\beta,\mathbf{k}}U^{*}_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}},

(490)

we obtain an eigen-problem

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\beta,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\beta,+-}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{V}_{\beta\alpha}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\end{array}\right).

Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle 2k_{B}T\tilde{\Psi}^{SM}_{+-}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\tilde{V}_{00}&0&\tilde{V}_{01}\\
\tilde{V}_{00}&0&\tilde{V}_{01}&0\\
0&\tilde{V}_{10}&0&\tilde{V}_{11}\\
\tilde{V}_{10}&0&\tilde{V}_{11}&0\end{array}\right)\tilde{\Psi}^{SM}_{+-},\;\;\tilde{\Psi}^{SM}_{+-}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{0,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{0,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{1,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{1,+-}\end{array}\right)

with

\tilde{V}_{00}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{Ab^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}},\;\tilde{V}_{01}=\tilde{V}_{10}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{\sqrt{AV_{0}}b^{2}k^{2}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}},\;\;\tilde{V}_{11}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{V_{0}k^{4}}{(k^{2}+b^{2})^{2}}.

(507)

We can see that
\tilde{V}_{00},\tilde{V}_{11},\tilde{V}_{01}>0
.
\tilde{V}_{00}
and
\tilde{V}_{11}
are just the same as the corresponding parameters for the intra-Chern-band pairing. The key difference is that
\tilde{V}_{01}
is now non-zero, so two channels are coupled with each other since both are s-wave.
We thus need to solve the whole
4\times 4
eigen-problem and the largest eigen-value gives
T_{c}
as

k_{B}T_{c}^{inter}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\tilde{V}_{00}+\tilde{V}_{11}+\sqrt{(\tilde{V}_{00}-\tilde{V}_{11})^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\right)

(508)

Compared to the intra-Chern-band pairing, one can show that

k_{B}T_{c}^{inter}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\tilde{V}_{00}+\tilde{V}_{11}+\sqrt{(\tilde{V}_{00}-\tilde{V}_{11})^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\right)>\frac{1}{2}\tilde{V}_{11}=k_{B}T_{c}^{intra}.

(509)

Numerical calculations give the same values for
\tilde{V}_{00}\approx 0.018meV
and
\tilde{V}_{11}\approx 0.387meV
. In addition, one finds
\tilde{V}_{01}\approx 0.042meV
. With all these values, the critical temperature for the inter-Chern-band channel

k_{B}T_{c}^{inter}\approx 0.196meV

(510)

is slightly larger than that of the intra-Chern-band channel (
\sim 0.193meV
).

Let us compare the
T_{c}
calculation from the linearized gap equation from the heavy fermion model with that from the numerical calculations of the full interaction model. We find that the inter-Chern-band s-wave pairing has a higher
T_{c}
than the intra-Chern-band d-wave pairing, which is the same for the heavy femrion model and the full interaction model. The heavy fermion model provides a simple explanation of this result. In the above derivation, one can see that both the
(U^{inter}_{0,\mathbf{k}})^{*}U^{inter}_{0,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
(U^{inter}_{1,\mathbf{k}})^{*}U^{inter}_{1,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
terms contribute to inter-Chern-band s-wave pairing, but only the
(U^{intra}_{1,\mathbf{k}})^{*}U^{intra}_{1,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
term contributes to the intra-Chern-band d-wave pairing (Here we add the label
inter
and
intra
to distinguish the interactions in different Chern-band channels). Since the interaction parameter in front of
(U^{inter}_{1,\mathbf{k}})^{*}U^{inter}_{1,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
term is the same as that for
(U^{intra}_{1,\mathbf{k}})^{*}U^{intra}_{1,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
term, the
T_{c}
of the inter-Chern-band s-wave pairing will always be larger than that of the intra-Chern-band d-wave pairing, as shown in Eq. (
509
).

The corresponding eigen-vector
\tilde{\Psi}^{SM}_{+-}
for the inter-Chern-band pairing takes the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}^{SM}_{+-}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{0,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{0,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{1,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{1,+-}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{N}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}2\tilde{V}_{01}\\
2\tilde{V}_{01}\\
\tilde{V}_{-}+\sqrt{\tilde{V}_{-}^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\\
\tilde{V}_{-}+\sqrt{\tilde{V}_{-}^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\end{array}\right),

where
\tilde{V}_{-}=-\tilde{V}_{00}+\tilde{V}_{11}
and
\mathcal{N}=2\sqrt{\tilde{V}_{-}^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\left(\tilde{V}_{-}+\sqrt{\tilde{V}_{-}^{2}+4\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}}\right)
.
This gives rise to the gap function

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta^{+,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\\
\Delta^{-,SM}_{\mathbf{k},+-}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k_{B}T_{c}^{inter}}\sum_{\alpha}U^{*}_{\alpha,\mathbf{k}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\Delta}^{-,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\\
\tilde{\Delta}^{+,SM}_{\alpha,+-}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(|\mathbf{k}|)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
1\end{array}\right),\;\;f(|\mathbf{k}|)=\frac{2\tilde{V}_{01}U_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{*}+(\tilde{V}_{-}+\sqrt{\tilde{V}_{-}^{2}+\tilde{V}_{01}^{2}})U_{1,\mathbf{k}}^{*}}{2k_{B}T_{c}^{inter}\sqrt{2\mathcal{N}}},

which is an s-wave pairing.

We can explicitly construct both singlet and triplet pairings as

\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=f(|\mathbf{k}|)(\zeta^{x})_{e_{1}e_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}^{00})_{s_{1}s_{2}}

for singlet pairing and

\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=f(|\mathbf{k}|)(\zeta^{y})_{e_{1}e_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}^{1M})_{s_{1}s_{2}}

for triplet pairing. Furthermore,
\hat{C}_{2x}
symmetry reverses the sign of
e_{Y}
on the Chern-band basis.
Since
(\zeta^{x})_{e_{1},-e_{1}}=(\zeta^{x})_{-e_{1},e_{1}}
and
(\zeta^{y})_{e_{1},-e_{1}}=-(\zeta^{y})_{-e_{1},e_{1}}
, the
\zeta^{x}

spin singlet channel should belong to
A_{1}
irrep while the
\zeta^{y}
spin triplet channel belongs to
A_{2}
irrep.

Figure 16:
(a) depicts the gap function
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},+-}
for the largest eigen-values of the linearized gap equation (
k_{B}T_{c}=0.21meV
) for
the inter-Chern-band channel. (c) shows the corresponding phase factor
\Phi_{+-}(\mathbf{k})/\pi
due to
C_{3z}
rotation for the gap function.

It should be emphasized that the degeneracy between singlet and triplet pairings for the inter-Chern-band channels only occurs in the chiral flat band limit. This degeneracy will be removed by a finite kinetic energy term. To see that, we need to go back to the LEG before taking the flat band approximation, namely Eqs. (
353
) and (
354
). Using the decomposition of the gap function (
397
) and
Tr(\zeta^{\nu}\zeta^{\mu})=2\delta_{\mu\nu}
, we have

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};\nu}^{\eta,SM}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2},\mu,e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left[P_{m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}\zeta^{\mu}P^{T}_{m_{2},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta}\right]_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{\nu})_{e_{2}e_{1}}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};\mu}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

(528)

With the form of projector operator
P
in Eq. (
352
), we find

P_{m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}\zeta^{\mu}P^{T}_{m_{2},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\zeta^{\mu}+\frac{d_{x,\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}}{|d_{x,\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}|}(m_{1}\zeta^{x}\zeta^{\mu}+m_{2}\zeta^{\mu}\zeta^{x})+m_{1}m_{2}\zeta^{x}\zeta^{\mu}\zeta^{x}\right).

(529)

Now let us focus on the inter-Chern-band channels, so
\mu,\nu=x,y
, and one can show, in this case, that only the terms
\zeta^{\mu}
and
\zeta^{x}\zeta^{\mu}\zeta^{x}
in
P_{m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-\eta}\zeta^{\mu}P^{T}_{m_{2},-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\eta}
can give a non-zero contribution to the LGE.
We first consider
\mu=x,\nu=y
, so the right-hand side of the LGE is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2},e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(\zeta^{x}+m_{1}m_{2}\zeta^{x}\right)_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{y})_{e_{2}e_{1}}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2},e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(1+m_{1}m_{2}\right)\delta_{e_{1},-e_{2}}(-i)e_{2}\delta_{e_{2},-e_{1}}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2},e_{1}}V^{\eta,e_{1},-e_{1}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(1+m_{1}m_{2}\right)ie_{1}\Delta^{-\eta,SM}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=0.

(530)

In the last step, we have used
\sum_{e_{1}}V^{\eta,e_{1},-e_{1}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}e_{1}=V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-V^{\eta,-+}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=0
.
Therefore,
\zeta^{x}
and
\zeta^{y}
channels can not be mixed with each other even when the kinetic energy term is included.

Next let’s consider the LGE for
\mu=\nu=x
(only for spin singlet), which can be simplified as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}^{\eta,00}=\frac{1}{4N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2},e_{1}e_{2}}V^{\eta,e_{1}e_{2}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(1+m_{1}m_{2})\zeta^{x}_{e_{1}e_{2}}(\zeta^{x})_{e_{2}e_{1}}\Delta^{-\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}m_{2}}V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(1+m_{1}m_{2})\Delta^{-\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{2}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{-\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}T^{\eta}_{++\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

(531)

in the chiral limit, where we have used
T^{\eta}_{-m_{1},-m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=T^{\eta}_{m_{1}m_{1}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
. With Eq. (
354
), we have

T^{\eta}_{++\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{\tanh(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}{2\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}},

(532)

and the LGE becomes

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}^{\eta,00}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\frac{\tanh(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}{\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\Delta^{-\eta,00}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};x}.

(533)

Similar derivation can be applied to
\mu=\nu=y
for spin triplet channel. One can show

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};y}^{\eta,1M}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m_{1}}V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Delta^{-\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};y}T^{\eta}_{m_{1},-m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

(534)

To evaluate
T^{\eta}_{m_{1},-m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, one should first keep a finite
\mu
and take
\mu\rightarrow 0
at the end of the calculation, which gives

T^{\eta}_{m_{1},-m_{1},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{\beta e^{\beta\xi_{m_{1},-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}}{(e^{\beta\xi_{m_{1},-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}+1)^{2}}.

(535)

Thus,

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};y}^{\eta,1M}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V^{\eta,+-}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\frac{\beta}{2\cosh^{2}(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}\Delta^{-\eta,1M}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime};y}.

(536)

Now one can see that the LGEs for
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}^{\eta,00}
and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};y}^{\eta,1M}
are different when the kinetic energy term is taken into account.
One can easily check that both LGEs (
533
) and (
536
) reduce to Eq. (
358
) in the flat band limit.
We may regard the band dispersion
\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
as a perturbation (much smaller than
T_{c}
) and then we can perform the perturbation expansion of the kinetic energy dependent terms.
We find

\frac{\tanh(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}{\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\approx\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{24}\beta^{3}\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{2}

(537)

and

\frac{\beta}{2\cosh^{2}(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}\approx\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{8}\beta^{3}\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{2}.

(538)

It is clear that the singlet channel
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}^{\eta,00}
has a stronger effective interaction compared to the triplet channel
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};y}^{\eta,1M}
as

\frac{\tanh(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}{\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}>\frac{\beta}{2\cosh^{2}(\beta\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}

(539)

in the limit
\xi_{+,-\eta,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\ll k_{B}T_{c}
. Thus, we expect the singlet channel
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}^{\eta,00}
has a higher
T_{c}
in the chiral non-flat limit for inter-Chern-band pairing channels.

VI.8.4
Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian, full self-consistent gap equation and ground state energy

The above linearized gap equation only gives the behavior of superconductivity near the critical temperature
T_{c}
. We hope to understand more
about the superconductivity property at zero temperature. For that purpose, we will derive the full self-consistent gap equation that works at zero temperature, as well as the ground state energy (or condensation energy) for superconductivity, based on the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian.

To do that, let’s first write down
the form of the BdG Hamiltonian on the Chern band basis,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{BdG}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in MBZ}\psi_{\pm,\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}H_{BdG}^{\pm}(\mathbf{k})\psi_{\pm,\mathbf{k}},\;\;\;

(540)

\displaystyle\psi_{+,\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}=(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\uparrow\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\uparrow\downarrow});\;\;\;\psi_{-,\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}=(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\uparrow\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\uparrow\downarrow})

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}^{\dagger}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{-}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{-}(\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}&-2\Delta_{-\mathbf{k}}^{T}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}^{T}\\
-2\Delta_{-\mathbf{k}}^{\star}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}^{\star}&-h_{+}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}\end{pmatrix}

where
h_{\pm}(\mathbf{k})
are the non-superconducting effective Hamiltonians for the flat bands at the valley
\eta=\pm
in TBG, respectively,
while
\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}=\Delta^{\eta=+}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},e_{2}}
.
H_{BdG}^{\pm}(\mathbf{k})
are 8-by-8 matrices.
The superconducting particle-hole symmetry
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
with
D(\hat{\mathcal{P}})=\rho_{x}\zeta_{0}s_{0}
, requires

-H_{BdG}^{-}(\mathbf{k})=\rho_{x}\zeta_{0}s_{0}H_{BdG}^{+\star}(-\mathbf{k})\rho_{x}\zeta_{0}s_{0},

(541)

where
s
,
\zeta
and
\rho
are the identity and Pauli matrices for the spin, Chern-band basis and superconducting particle-hole basis.
One should note that the superconducting particle-hole operator
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
is different from the unitary particle-hole symmetry operator
\hat{P}

for the non-superconducting TBG Hamiltonian.

It should be noted that
h_{\eta=\pm}(\mathbf{k})
is a 2-by-2 matrix and does
not
possess a diagonal form on the Chern-band basis.
From the relation (
195
) between the eigen-state basis and Chern-band basis, we can show that

h_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})=(d_{0,\eta}(\mathbf{k})-\mu)\zeta^{0}+d_{x,\eta}(\mathbf{k})\zeta^{x},

(542)

where

d_{0,\eta}(\mathbf{k})=(\epsilon_{+,\eta}(\mathbf{k})+\epsilon_{-,\eta}(\mathbf{k}))/2;\;\;d_{x,\eta}(\mathbf{k})=(\epsilon_{+,\eta}(\mathbf{k})-\epsilon_{-,\eta}(\mathbf{k}))/2,

(543)

and
\epsilon_{n,\eta}(\mathbf{k})
are the single-particle eigen-energies of the BM model
\hat{H}_{0}
so they should be real.
Time reversal symmetry requires

\epsilon_{n,\eta}(\mathbf{k})=\epsilon_{n,-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})\implies d_{0,-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})=d_{0,\eta}(\mathbf{k});\;d_{x,-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})=d_{x,\eta}(\mathbf{k})\implies h_{-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})=h_{\eta}(\mathbf{k}).

(544)

As discussed above, the spin-singlet and triplet channels are decoupled and below we discuss the singlet and triplet channels, separately.
We first consider the spin-singlet channels, for which the gap function in
H_{BdG}^{+}(\mathbf{k})
has the form

\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes(is_{y}),\text{or}\;\;\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}},

(545)

where
\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}
is a
2\times 2
matrix on the Chern-band basis.

Since the single-particle Hamiltonian is diagonal in spin space, the Hamiltonian
H_{BdG}^{+}(\mathbf{k})
with spin-singlet pairing has a block diagonal form

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes is_{y}\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes(-is_{y})&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&0&0&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\\
0&h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&-2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}&0\\
0&-2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})&0\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&0&0&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\end{pmatrix}

(546)

on the basis

(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,\uparrow},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,\uparrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,\downarrow})
.
Thus, we can consider two blocks separately,

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+,\lambda=\pm}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&2\lambda\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\\
2\lambda\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\end{pmatrix}.

(547)

Both blocks are 4-by-4 matrices, written on the basis
(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\uparrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\downarrow})

and
(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\uparrow})
for the
\lambda=+
and
-
blocks, separately.

To determine the exact pairing form for the temperature well below
T_{c}
, we need to go back to the full form of the gap equation (
333
). Since
\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}}
for spin singlet, we have
\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\uparrow,e_{2}\downarrow}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}
and
\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\downarrow,e_{2}\uparrow}=-\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}
.
The gap equation the explicitly reads

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=-\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\downarrow,e_{2}\uparrow}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2},+,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},-,\downarrow}\rangle

(548)

and

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\uparrow,e_{2}\downarrow}=-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2}+\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1}-\uparrow}\rangle.

(549)

Let’s look at the first equation and we need to evaluate
\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},+,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},-,\downarrow}\rangle
from the BdG Hamiltonian
H_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})
since

\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\uparrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\downarrow})[H_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})]_{e_{1},e_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{2},+,\uparrow}\\
\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2},-,\downarrow}\end{array}\right).

(550)

Now let’s define

U_{\mathbf{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{k}}&v_{\mathbf{k}}\\
w_{\mathbf{k}}&r_{\mathbf{k}}\end{pmatrix},\;\;H_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})=U_{\mathbf{k}}D_{\mathbf{k}}U^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}

(551)

where
u_{\mathbf{k}},v_{\mathbf{k}},w_{\mathbf{k}},r_{\mathbf{k}}
are two-by-two matrices and
D_{\mathbf{k}}
is a diagonal matrix,
D_{\mathbf{k}}=\text{Diag}(E^{+}_{1},E^{+}_{2},E^{-}_{1},E^{-}_{2})
, with
E^{+}_{n}>0
and
E^{-}_{n}<0
(
n=1,2
). Now let’s define Bogoliubov quasi-particle operators

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\uparrow}\\
\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\downarrow}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{n}[U_{\mathbf{k}}]_{e_{1}n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}\\
\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n}\end{array}\right)

(552)

so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,+}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}(\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}}\;\;\beta_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}})[D_{\mathbf{k}}]_{e_{1},e_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}}\\
\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}\left(E^{+}_{\mathbf{k},n}\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}+(-E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n})\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n}\beta_{-\mathbf{k},n}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}.

(556)

The ground state of superconductors
|GS\rangle
should be defined by
\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}\left|GS\right\rangle=\beta_{\mathbf{k},n}\left|GS\right\rangle=0

with
n=1,2
, and
\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
and
\beta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
create Bogoliubov quasi-particles with the energy
E^{+}_{\mathbf{k},n}
and
-E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}
,
respectively, both of which are positive numbers.
More explicitly, we have

\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\uparrow}=\sum_{n}(u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}+v_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n});\;\;\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\downarrow}=\sum_{n}(w_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}+r_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n}).

(557)

From the second equation, we can obtain

\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\downarrow}=\sum_{n}(w^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}+r^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}\beta_{-\mathbf{k},n}).

(558)

Now we can substitute these expressions into the gap equation (
548
) and obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2},+,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},-,\downarrow}\rangle

(559)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},nm}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle(u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}\alpha_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}+v_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}\beta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n})(w^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}m}\alpha^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}+r^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}m}\beta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\left(u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}(1-f(E^{+}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}))+v_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}r^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}f(-E^{-}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n})\right),

where
f(E)=(e^{\beta E}+1)^{-1}
is the Fermi distribution function.
At zero temperature, the self-consistent gap equation reads

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}.

(560)

This self-consistent gap equation can be applied to both intra-Chern-band and inter-Chern-band channels for spin singlet state.

Similar procedure can be applied to the gap equation (
549
) and the BdG Hamiltonian
H_{BdG}^{+,\lambda=-}(\mathbf{k})
.
From the form of the BdG Hamiltonian in Eq. (
547
), we notice that the
\lambda=\pm
Hamiltonians are related to each other by a unitary transformation,

H_{BdG}^{+,-}(\mathbf{k})=\rho_{z}H_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})\rho_{z}.

(561)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,-}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\uparrow})[H_{BdG}^{+,-}(\mathbf{k})]_{e_{1},e_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{2},+,\downarrow}\\
\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2},-,\uparrow}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\uparrow})[\rho_{z}U_{\mathbf{k}}D_{\mathbf{k}}U^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}\rho_{z}]_{e_{1},e_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{2},+,\downarrow}\\
\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2},-,\uparrow}\end{array}\right).

Thus, the eigen-energies of
H_{BdG}^{+,-}(\mathbf{k})
are the same as those of
H_{BdG}^{+,+}(\mathbf{k})
, while the Bogoliubov quasi-particle operators are
defined by

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},e_{1},+,\downarrow}\\
-\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1},-,\uparrow}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{n}[U_{\mathbf{k}}]_{e_{1}n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}\\
\beta^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n}\end{array}\right)

(568)

with the same
U
-matrix as in Eq. (
551
). With the above transformation, we find

\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2}+\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1}-\uparrow}\rangle=-\sum_{n}\left(u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}^{*}(1-f(E^{+}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}))+v_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}r^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}f(-E^{-}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n})\right)

(569)

and thus the gap equation (
549
) transforms into

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2}+\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1}-\uparrow}\rangle

(570)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\left(u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}^{*}(1-f(E^{+}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}))+v_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}r^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}f(-E^{-}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n})\right),

which is the same as Eq. (
559
). Thus, both blocks give the same gap equation, which is required for the consistency of the theory.

We can also calculate the ground state energy or the condensation energy of superconductivity based on the current BdG formalism.
The BdG Hamiltonian in Eq. (
540
) only contains the fermion bilinear terms, and to get the correct form of the ground state energy, one needs to keep all the constant terms for the Hamiltonian, which reads

\mathcal{H}_{tot}=\sum_{\eta=\pm}\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{BdG}+\sum_{\mathbf{k},\eta,e}[h_{-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})]_{ee}-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}.

(571)

Our goal is to calculate the condensation energy, which is defined as

E_{c}=\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{s}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{n},

(572)

where
\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{s}=\langle GS|\mathcal{H}_{tot}|GS\rangle
is the superconductor ground state energy, and

\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{n}
is the energy of the normal metallic state, which can be obtained from
\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{s}
by
taking the superconductor gap function
\Delta
to be zero. As the last two terms in Eq. (
571
) are constants, we first focus on calculating

\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{\eta}\rangle_{s}
. As shown in Eq. (
546
),
H^{+}_{BdG}
has a block-digonal form and thus we expect

\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+}\rangle_{s}=\sum_{\lambda=\pm}\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,\lambda}\rangle_{s}
.
In Eq. (
VI.8.4
), we rewrite
\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,+}
in terms of quadratic form of Bogoliubov quasi-particle operators
\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n},\beta_{\mathbf{k},n}
. Since the superconductor ground state is defined by
\alpha_{\mathbf{k},n}\left|GS\right\rangle=\beta_{\mathbf{k},n}\left|GS\right\rangle=0
, we obtain

\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,+}\rangle_{s}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}.

(573)

Furthermore, as the
\lambda=-
block is related to
\lambda=+
block by a unitary transformation (
561
)
and thus the ground state energy of these two blocks are the same,
\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,-}\rangle_{s}=\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+,+}\rangle_{s}
.
In addition, one can show that
\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{\eta=-}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{\eta=+}
in the second quantization form, so

\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{-}\rangle_{s}=\langle\mathcal{H}_{BdG}^{+}\rangle_{s}
.

The second term in Eq. (
571
) is related to single-particle Hamiltonian and from Eq. (
542
), we obtain

\sum_{\mathbf{k},\eta,e}[h_{-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})]_{ee}=2\sum_{\mathbf{k},\eta}(d_{0,-\eta}(-\mathbf{k})-\mu)=-4\mu N_{M}+\sum_{\mathbf{k},\eta}(\epsilon_{+,\eta}(\mathbf{k})+\epsilon_{-,\eta}(\mathbf{k}))=-4\mu N_{M},

(574)

where we have used the property of unitary particle-hole symmetry that requires
\epsilon_{+,\eta}(\mathbf{k})=-\epsilon_{-,\eta}(-\mathbf{k})
.

Now let’s look at the last term, for which we need to evaluate
\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle
.
For spin singlet channel, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=-\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}(\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}+\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}\left((\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\downarrow}\rangle-\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\uparrow}\rangle)\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}+(\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\downarrow}\rangle-\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\uparrow}\rangle)\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\right)

where we have used
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}(is_{y})_{s_{1}s_{2}}
for spin singlet, so that
\Delta^{\pm}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\uparrow,e_{2}\downarrow}=\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}
and
\Delta^{\pm}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\downarrow,e_{2}\uparrow}=-\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}
.
With the Bogoliubov transformation (
552
) and (
568
), we find

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\downarrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}v_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(575)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\uparrow}\rangle=-\sum_{n}v^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(576)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\downarrow}\rangle=-\sum_{n}w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(577)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\uparrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n},

(578)

which leads to

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}\left(v_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}-w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\right)

(579)

The unitary property
U_{\mathbf{k}}U^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}=I
(
I
is an identity matrix) can lead to

\sum_{n}v^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}=-\sum_{n}u^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(580)

and together with
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{-}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}e_{1}}
for spin-singlet channel, we eventually obtain

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

(581)

The superconductor ground state energy is given by

\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{s}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}-4\mu N_{M}+4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}.

(582)

and the condensation energy is

E_{c}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}(E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}-\xi^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n})+4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}},

(583)

where
\xi^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}
is the eigen-energy of the BdG Hamiltonian at zero gap function
\Delta
.

For spin-triplet channel, we consider the unitary pairing
(\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes{\mathcal{S}})^{\dagger}(\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes{\mathcal{S}})\propto I
, and in this case, we can always rotate the spin basis of gap function to
{\mathcal{S}}^{10}=s_{x}
since the single-particle Hamiltonian part is spin-independent. For the triplet component
{\mathcal{S}}^{10}=s_{x}
, the BdG Hamiltonian for one valley reads

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\otimes s_{x}\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes s_{x}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\otimes s_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&0&0&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\\
0&h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}&0\\
0&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})&0\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&0&0&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\end{pmatrix}

(584)

on the basis

(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,\uparrow},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,\uparrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,\downarrow})
. This Hamiltonian is also block diagonal and we rewrite each block Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+,\lambda=\pm}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})&2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}\\
2\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&-h_{-}^{\star}(-\mathbf{k})\end{pmatrix}.

(585)

The basis functions are
(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\uparrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\downarrow})

and
(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,+,s=\downarrow},\gamma_{-\mathbf{k},e_{Y}=\pm,-,s=\uparrow})
for
\lambda=+
and
-
, separately.
Compared to the spin-singlet state, the only difference is that the gap function here is independent of
\lambda
. Thus, all the derivation above
can be directly applied here.

For spin triplet state,
\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}(s_{x})_{s_{1}s_{2}}
, so
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\uparrow,e_{2}\downarrow}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\downarrow,e_{2}\uparrow}
.
The gap equation the explicitly reads

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\downarrow,e_{2}\uparrow}=-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2},+,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},-,\downarrow}\rangle

(586)

and

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}\uparrow,e_{2}\downarrow}=-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2}+\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1}-\uparrow}\rangle.

(587)

Now one can see that for
\lambda=\pm
, both the BdG Hamiltonian and the gap equation are completely the same. Thus, we only need to look at one of them. Following the procedure above, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\langle\gamma_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{2},+,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}e_{1},-,\downarrow}\rangle

(588)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}\left(u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}(1-f(E^{+}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}))+v_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}r^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}f(-E^{-}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n})\right),

At zero temperature, the self-consistent gap equation for spin-triplet channels reads

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=-\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}V_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{+e_{1}e_{2}}u_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{2}n}w^{*}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},e_{1}n}.

(589)

Although the above formalism looks similar to the spin singlet case, the form of the gap function is different.
As discussed in the paragraph after the expansion (
397
) of the gap function, we know that for the inter-Chern-band channel,
the gap function takes the form
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k};y}(\zeta^{y})_{e_{1}e_{2}}
for even parity (
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=+1
)
and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k};x}(\zeta^{x})_{e_{1}e_{2}}
for odd parity (
\lambda_{C_{2z}}=-1
) so that the condition (
396
)
(
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=-\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{2},e_{1}}
) is satisfied.

For spin-triplet pairing, since the spin part is symmetric,
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=\sum_{\mu}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};\mu}\zeta^{\mu}
needs to satisfy
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}=-\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{2},e_{1}}
according to Eq. (
396
)
where
\lambda_{C_{2z}}
is the eigen-value of
C_{2z}
operator acting on the gap function.
This requires either anti-symmetric
\zeta^{\mu=y}
and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};\mu=y}=\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta_{-\mathbf{k};\mu=y}
for inter-Chern-band pairing,
or symmetry
\zeta^{\mu=0,x,z}
and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};\mu=0,x,z}=-\lambda_{C_{2z}}\Delta_{-\mathbf{k};\mu=0,x,z}
for both intra-Chern-band pairing (
\mu=0,z
) and inter-Chern-band pairing (
\mu=x
).

For the ground state energy, we expect the first and second terms in Eq. (
571
) should still take the same form once the eigen-energy
E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}
is calculated from the BdG Hamiltonian (
585
) with a different form of
\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}
.
For the last term, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=-\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}(\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}+\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}e_{2}}\left((\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\downarrow}\rangle+\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\uparrow}\rangle)\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}+(\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\downarrow}\rangle+\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\uparrow}\rangle)\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\right)

where we have used
\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1},e_{2}s_{2}}=\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1},e_{2}}(s_{x})_{s_{1}s_{2}}
.
We can again apply the Bogoliubov transformation (
552
) and (
568
) as the Hamiltonian (
585
) is the same as (
547
) for
\lambda=+
. We find

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\downarrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}v_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(590)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}+\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\uparrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}v^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(591)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\downarrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(592)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}-\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}+\uparrow}\rangle=\sum_{n}w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n},

(593)

which leads to

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}\left(v_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}+w_{-\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}u^{*}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta^{-}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}\right)

(594)

From the unitary property

\sum_{n}v^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}r_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}=-\sum_{n}u^{*}_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}

(595)

(
U_{\mathbf{k}}U^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}=I
)
and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}=-\Delta^{-}_{-\mathbf{k},e_{2}e_{1}}
for spin-triplet channel, we obtain

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathbf{k}\eta e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}\langle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}\eta s_{1}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}e_{2}-\eta s_{2}}\rangle\Delta^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}s_{1}e_{2}s_{2}}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}

(596)

The superconductor ground state energy is given by

\langle\mathcal{H}_{tot}\rangle_{s}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}-4\mu N_{M}+4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}}.

(597)

and the condensation energy is

E_{c}=4\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}(E^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}-\xi^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n})+4\sum_{\mathbf{k}e_{1}e_{2}n}u_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}n}^{*}w_{\mathbf{k},e_{2}n}\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{1}e_{2}},

(598)

where
\xi^{-}_{\mathbf{k},n}
is the eigen-energy of the BdG Hamiltonian at zero gap function
\Delta
.
So we find the same form of the ground state energy for spin-triplet pairing as that of spin-singlet pairing.

VI.8.5
Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian for the Intra-Chern-band Channel

The linearized gap equation has shown the form of the spin-singlet intra-Chern-band pairing channels in Eq. (
VI.8.2
) and (
VI.8.2
) around
T_{c}
, which belong to 2D
E_{2}
irrep. There are degenerate pairing channels, and this degeneracy is expected to break when the temperature is below
T_{c}
. So here we apply the BdG formalism to the intra-Chern-band pairing channels to study the pairing state at zero temperature. We keep the general form

(\Delta_{\mathbf{k}})_{e_{1}e_{2}}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{1}}\delta_{e_{1}e_{2}}is_{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}&0\\
0&\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}\end{array}\right)is_{y}=(\Delta_{\mathbf{k},0}\zeta^{0}+\Delta_{\mathbf{k},z}\zeta^{z})_{e_{1},e_{2}}is_{y}.

(599)

For the
\hat{C}_{2z}\hat{T}
-invariant pairing, the intra-Chern-band gap function need to satisfies

\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}=(\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-})^{\star}\implies\Delta_{\mathbf{k},0}=\Delta_{\mathbf{k},0}^{\star};\Delta_{\mathbf{k},z}=-\Delta_{\mathbf{k},z}^{\star}

(600)

according to the constraint (
VI.7.1
).

With the single-particle Hamiltonian (
542
), the BdG Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H_{BdG}^{+,\lambda}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}(d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu)\zeta^{0}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{x}&2\lambda(\Delta_{0,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{0}+\Delta_{z,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{z})\\
2\lambda(\Delta^{\star}_{0,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{0}+\Delta^{\star}_{z,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{z})&-(d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu)\zeta^{0}-d_{x,\mathbf{k}}\zeta^{x}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu&d_{x,\mathbf{k}}&2\lambda\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}&0\\
d_{x,\mathbf{k}}&d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu&0&2\lambda\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}\\
2\lambda\Delta^{\star}_{\mathbf{k},+}&0&-(d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu)&-d_{x,\mathbf{k}}\\
0&2\lambda\Delta^{\star}_{\mathbf{k},-}&-d_{x,\mathbf{k}}&-(d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu)\end{pmatrix},

(601)

where
\lambda=\pm
,
d_{0(x),\mathbf{k}}=d_{0(x),+}(\mathbf{k})
, and
\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}=\Delta_{0,\mathbf{k}}+e_{Y}\Delta_{z,\mathbf{k}},\;e_{Y}=\pm

for two intra-Chern-band channels with the Chern number
e_{Y}
.
Below we mainly focus on two types of pairing forms: (1) the Euler pairing with
|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|
Yu et al. 2022a
, which breaks three-fold rotation and thus is a nematic phase, and (2) the chiral d-wave pairing with
\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}\neq 0,\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}=0
or
\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}\neq 0,\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}=0
, which respect three-fold rotation, but break TR.

We first want to discuss the general conditions for the existence of point nodes in the BdG Hamiltonian (
VI.8.5
). The eigen-energy
E^{\pm}_{\mathbf{k},n}
of the BdG Hamiltonian (
VI.8.5
) can be solved explicitly for two independent order parameters
\Delta_{+,\mathbf{k}}

and
\Delta_{-,\mathbf{k}}
,

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{\mathbf{k},n}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{A+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+n\sqrt{A^{2}-4B^{2}+\tilde{d}^{2}_{0,\mathbf{k}}d^{2}_{x,\mathbf{k}}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}(A-2B\cos(\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}))}}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2A+\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+2n\sqrt{A^{2}-4B^{2}+\tilde{d}^{2}_{0,\mathbf{k}}d^{2}_{x,\mathbf{k}}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}(A-2B\cos(\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}))}}

(602)

where
n=\pm
,
\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}=d_{0,\mathbf{k}}-\mu
,
A=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|^{2}+|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|^{2}
and
B=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|
. We also denote

\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}|e^{i\Phi_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}}.

(603)

From the linearized gap equation discussed in Sec.
VI.8.2
, we have shown that the inter-Chern-band channel will favor d-wave
E_{2}
pairing with and the gap function ansatz

\Delta_{\mathbf{k};e_{Y}}=\Delta_{e_{Y}}\frac{k^{2}_{-e_{Y}}}{k^{2}+b^{2}},

(604)

where
e_{Y}=\pm
and
\Delta_{e_{Y}}=|\Delta_{e_{Y}}|e^{i\varphi_{e_{Y}}}
. With this gap function ansatz, we find

\Phi_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}=\varphi_{e_{Y}}-2e_{Y}\theta_{\mathbf{k}}

(605)

with the momentum
\mathbf{k}=k(\cos\theta_{\mathbf{k}},\sin\theta_{\mathbf{k}})
and

|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},e_{Y}}|=|\Delta_{e_{Y}}|\frac{k^{2}}{k^{2}+b^{2}}.

(606)

For the above eigen-energy, the gapless points at zero energy require

\displaystyle(2A+\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2})^{2}=4(A^{2}-4B^{2}+\tilde{d}^{2}_{0,\mathbf{k}}d^{2}_{x,\mathbf{k}}+d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}(A-2B\cos(\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+})))

\displaystyle\rightarrow 16B^{2}+4A\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}+(\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}-d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2})^{2}+8Bd_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}\cos(\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+})=0

\displaystyle\rightarrow(4B+\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}-d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2})^{2}+4\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}(|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|-|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|)^{2}+16Bd_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}}{2}\right)=0.

(607)

where we have used
A-2B=(|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|-|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|)^{2}
. One can see that all three terms in the above equation have to be equal or larger than 0, so the above equation can be satisfied for the following conditions, either (1)

\displaystyle 4B+\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}=d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2};

(608)

\displaystyle|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|;

(609)

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}}{2}\right)=0,

(610)

or (2)

\displaystyle 4B=d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2};

(611)

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}=0;

(612)

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}}{2}\right)=0.

(613)

For the case (1), we find that Eq. (
609
) is just the condition of Euler pairing. Let us define
|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},+}|=|\Delta_{\mathbf{k},-}|=\tilde{\Delta}_{0,k}
and then Eq. (
608
) is simplified to

4\tilde{\Delta}_{0,k}^{2}+\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}^{2}=d_{x,\mathbf{k}}^{2},

(614)

which determines the the amplitude of the momentum for nodes. In addition, Eq. (
610
) requires

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}}{2}\right)=0\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{k},-}-\Phi_{\mathbf{k},+}=4\theta_{\mathbf{k}}+\varphi_{-}-\varphi_{+}=(2n+1)\pi,

\displaystyle\rightarrow\theta_{\mathbf{k}}=\frac{1}{4}((2n+1)\pi+\varphi_{+}-\varphi_{-})

(615)

with
n\in\mathcal{Z}
(any integer number), which fixes the momentum angle
\theta_{\mathbf{k}}
for the location of the nodes in the 2D momentum space. Thus, we generally have point nodes in the 2D momentum space for the Hamiltonian (
VI.8.5
), with their locations fixed by Eqs. (
614
) and (
VI.8.5
).

For the case (2), both Eqs. (
611
) and (
612
) determine the required momentum amplitude for the nodes, which can not be satisfied in general. However, in the chiral limit
d_{0,\mathbf{k}}=0
and with zero chemical potential
\mu=0
,
\tilde{d}_{0,\mathbf{k}}=0
for any momentum. In this case, Eq. (
611
